# Quebec

This article is about the Canadian province. For the province's capital city, see [Quebec City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_City). For the historical province, see [Province of Quebec (1763–1791)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Quebec_\(1763%E2%80%931791\)). For other uses, see [Quebec (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_\(disambiguation\)).

**Quebec**[^cite_note-11] ([French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language): *Québec*)[^cite_note-13] is [Canada](5042916.md)'s [largest province by area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Canadian_provinces_and_territories_by_area).[^cite_note-14][^cite_note-15] Located in [Central Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Canada), it is the country's only [Francophone-majority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_in_Canada) province. It is home to speakers of [Québécois French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_French). It shares borders with [Ontario](22218.md) to the west, [Newfoundland and Labrador](21980.md) to the northeast, [New Brunswick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Brunswick) to the southeast and a coastal border with the territory of [Nunavut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nunavut). To the south, it borders the United States.[^cite_note-16] Quebec has a population of around eight million, making it Canada's [second-most populous province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_of_Canada_by_province_and_territory), behind Ontario.

Between 1534 and 1763, what became Quebec was the [French colony of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_\(New_France\)) and was the most developed colony in [New France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_France). Following the [Seven Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Years'_War), Canada became a [British colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_evolution_of_the_British_Empire#List_of_territories_that_were_once_a_part_of_the_British_Empire), first as the [Province of Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Quebec_\(1763%E2%80%931791\)) (1763–1791), then [Lower Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Canada) (1791–1841), and lastly part of the [Province of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Canada) (1841–1867) as a result of the [Lower Canada Rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Canada_Rebellion). It was [confederated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Confederation) with Ontario, [Nova Scotia](21184.md), and New Brunswick in 1867. [Until the early 1960s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grande_Noirceur), the [Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Canada) played a large role in Quebec's social and cultural institutions. However, the [Quiet Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quiet_Revolution) of the 1960s to 1980s increased the role of the [Government of Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Quebec) in *[l'État québécois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89tat_qu%C3%A9b%C3%A9cois)* (the public authority of Quebec).

The Government of Quebec functions within the context of Canada's [Westminster system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westminster_system) and is both a [liberal democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_democracy) and a [constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy). The [Premier of Quebec](370751.md) is [head of government](37570.md). [Independence debates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_sovereignty_movement) have played a large role in [Quebec politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_politics). Quebec society's [cohesion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_order) and [specificity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distinct_society) is based on three of its unique [statutory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute) documents: the *[Quebec Charter of Human Rights and Freedoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_Charter_of_Human_Rights_and_Freedoms)*, the *[Charter of the French Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charter_of_the_French_Language)*, and the *[Civil Code of Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Code_of_Quebec)*. Furthermore, unlike the other provinces which use a [common-law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_law) system, [law in Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_law) is mixed: [private law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_law) is exercised under a [civil law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_law_\(legal_system\)) system, while [public law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_law) is exercised under a common-law system.

The [economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Quebec) is mainly supported by its large service sector and varied industrial sector. Its exports are mainly [aeronautics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aeronautics), [hydroelectricity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydro-Qu%C3%A9bec), mining, [pharmaceuticals](560876.md), aluminum, wood, and paper. Quebec is well known for producing [maple syrup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maple_syrup), for [its comedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comedy_in_Quebec), and for making [ice hockey](14790.md) one of Canada's [most popular sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_hockey_in_Canada). The province's [cultural products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Quebec) include [literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_literature), [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Quebec), [films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Quebec), [TV shows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_French-language_Canadian_television_series), and [festivals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_festivals_in_Quebec).

## Etymology

The name *Québec* comes from an [Algonquin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algonquin_language) word meaning 'narrow passage' or 'strait'.[^cite_note-17] The name originally referred to the area around [Quebec City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_City) where the [Saint Lawrence River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Lawrence_River) narrows. Early variations in the spelling included *Québecq* and *Kébec*.[^cite_note-18] French explorer [Samuel de Champlain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_de_Champlain) chose the name *Québec* in 1608 for the colonial outpost he chose as the [administrative seat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_seat) for [New France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_France).[^cite_note-19]

## History

Main article: [History of Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Quebec)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of Quebec history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Quebec_history).

### Indigenous peoples and European expeditions (pre-1608)

Main articles: [Peopling of the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peopling_of_the_Americas), [Indigenous peoples in Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_in_Quebec), and [Exploration of North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exploration_of_North_America)



A depiction of [Jacques Cartier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Cartier) by [Théophile Hamel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Th%C3%A9ophile_Hamel), 1844

The [Paleo-Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleo-Indians) were the first to establish themselves in what became Quebec, arriving after the [Laurentide Ice Sheet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurentide_Ice_Sheet) melted roughly 11,000 years ago.[^cite_note-20][^cite_note-21] By the time of the European explorations of the 1500s, eleven [Indigenous peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_in_Quebec): the [Inuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inuit) and ten [First Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Nations_in_Canada) – the [Abenaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abenaki), [Algonquin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algonquin_people) (or Anichinabés), [Atikamekw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atikamekw), [Cree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cree), [Huron-Wendat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wendat_people), [Wolastoqiyik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolastoqiyik), [Miꞌkmaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mi%EA%9E%8Ckmaq), [Iroquois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iroquois), [Innu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Innu) and [Naskapi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naskapi) had formed.[^cite_note-22] Algonquians organized into seven political entities and lived as nomads,surviving by hunting, gathering, and fishing.[^cite_note-23] Inuit fished and hunted whales and seals along the coasts of Hudson and Ungava Bays.[^cite_note-Marsh19882-24]

In the 15th century, the [Byzantine Empire fell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople), prompting Western Europeans to search for sea routes to the [Far East](11672.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECharpentierDurocherLavilleLinteau198547-25] As such, around 1522–23, [Giovanni da Verrazzano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giovanni_da_Verrazzano) persuaded [King Francis I of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Francis_I_of_France) to commission an expedition to find a western route to [Cathay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathay) (China) via a [Northwest Passage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Passage). Though this expedition was unsuccessful, it bestowed the name *New France* on northeast North America.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECharpentierDurocherLavilleLinteau198550-26] In his first expedition ordered from the Kingdom of France, [Jacques Cartier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Cartier) became the first European explorer to discover and map the Quebec area when he landed in [Gaspé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gasp%C3%A9,_Quebec) on July 24, 1534.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERiendeau200736-27] In the second expedition, in 1535, Cartier explored the lands of [Stadacona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stadacona) and named the village and its surrounding territories *[Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_\(New_France\))* (from *kanata*, 'village' in [Iroquois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iroquoian_language)). Cartier returned to France with about 10 [St. Lawrence Iroquoians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Lawrence_Iroquoians), including Chief [Donnacona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donnacona). In 1540, Donnacona recounted to Francis the legend of the [Kingdom of Saguenay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Saguenay), inspiring him to order a third expedition, this time led by [Jean-François de La Rocque de Roberval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Fran%C3%A7ois_de_La_Rocque_de_Roberval); it failed to find the kingdom.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECharpentierDurocherLavilleLinteau198551-28]

After these expeditions, France mostly abandoned North America for 50 years because of its financial crisis; France was involved in the [Italian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Wars) and religious wars.[^cite_note-29] Around 1580, the rise of the [fur trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fur_trade) reignited French interest; New France became a [colonial trading post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Factory_\(trading_post\)).[^cite_note-mathieu-30] In 1603, [Samuel de Champlain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_de_Champlain) travelled to the Saint Lawrence River and, on Pointe Saint-Mathieu, established a [defence pact](4991894.md) with the Innu, Wolastoqiyik and Mi'kmaq, that would be "a decisive factor in the maintenance of a French colonial enterprise in America despite an enormous numerical disadvantage vis-à-vis the British".[^cite_note-Litalien20042-31] Thus began French military support to the Algonquian and Huron peoples against Iroquois attacks; these became known as the [Iroquois Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaver_Wars) and lasted from the early 1600s to the early 1700s.[^cite_note-32]

### New France (1608–1763)

Main articles: [Canada (New France)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_\(New_France\)) and [New France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_France)



Three Huron-Wendat chiefs from [Wendake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wendake,_Quebec). New France had largely peaceful relations with the Indigenous people, such as their allies the Huron. After the [defeat of the Huron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beaver_Wars) by their mutual enemy, the [Iroquois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iroquois), many fled from Ontario to Quebec.

In 1608, Samuel de Champlain[^cite_note-33] returned to the region as head of an exploration party. On July 3, 1608, with the support of King [Henry IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henri_IV), he founded the [Habitation de Québec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habitation_de_Qu%C3%A9bec) (now Quebec City) and made it the capital of New France.[^cite_note-mathieu-30] The settlement became a permanent [fur trading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fur_trading) outpost, where First Nations traded furs for French goods, such as metal objects, guns, alcohol, and clothing.[^cite_note-LeaMilward20012-34] Missionary groups arrived after the founding of Quebec City. [Coureurs des bois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coureurs_des_bois) and [Catholic missionaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_missions) used canoes to explore the interior and establish fur trading forts.[^cite_note-Poulter20102-35][^cite_note-Chartrand2013b2-36]

The [Compagnie des Cent-Associés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compagnie_des_Cent-Associ%C3%A9s), which had been granted a royal mandate to manage New France in 1627, introduced the [Custom of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Custom_of_Paris_in_New_France) and the [seigneurial system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seigneurial_system_of_New_France), and forbade settlement by anyone other than [Catholics](606848.md).[^cite_note-Harris19842-37] In 1629, [Quebec City surrendered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surrender_of_Quebec), without battle, to English [privateers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privateer) during the [Anglo-French War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-French_War_\(1627%E2%80%9329\)); in 1632, the English king agreed to return it with the [Treaty of Saint-Germain-en-Laye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Saint-Germain-en-Laye_\(1632\)). [Trois-Rivières](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trois-Rivi%C3%A8res) was founded at de Champlain's request in 1634.[^cite_note-38] [Paul de Chomedey de Maisonneuve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_de_Chomedey,_Sieur_de_Maisonneuve) founded [Ville-Marie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Ville-Marie) (now Montreal) in 1642.

In 1663, the Company of New France ceded Canada to King [Louis XIV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XIV), who made New France into a royal province.[^cite_note-rois-39] New France became a [true colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_French_colonial_empire) administered by the [Sovereign Council of New France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovereign_Council_of_New_France) from Quebec City. A [governor-general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_New_France) governed Canada and its administrative dependencies: Acadia, [Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_\(New_France\)) and Plaisance.[^cite_note-Hayes20082-40] French settlers were mostly farmers and known as "[Canadiens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadiens)" or "[Habitants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habitants)". Though immigration was modest,[^cite_note-Preston20092-41] the colony grew via the Habitants' high birth rates.[^cite_note-Powell20092-42][^cite_note-McIlwraithMuller20012-43] In 1665, the [Carignan-Salières regiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C3%A9giment_de_Carignan-Sali%C3%A8res) developed the string of fortifications known as the "Valley of Forts" to protect against Iroquois invasions and brought with them 1,200 new men.[^cite_note-44] To redress the gender imbalance and boost population growth, King [Louis XIV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XIV) sponsored the passage of approximately 800 young French women ([King's Daughters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_filles_du_roi)) to the colony.[^cite_note-rois-39] In 1666, intendant [Jean Talon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Talon) organized the first census and counted 3,215 Habitants. Talon enacted policies to diversify agriculture and encourage births, which, by 1672, had increased the population to 6,700.[^cite_note-45]

New France's territory grew to extend from [Hudson Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_Bay) to the [Gulf of Mexico](21076367.md), and encompassed the [Great Lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes).[^cite_note-46] In the early 1700s, [Governor Callières](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis-Hector_de_Calli%C3%A8re) concluded the [Great Peace of Montreal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Peace_of_Montreal), which confirmed the alliance between the Algonquian and New France and definitively ended the Iroquois Wars.[^cite_note-47] From 1688 onwards, the competition between the French and British to control North America's interior and monopolize fur trade pitted New France and its indigenous allies against the Iroquois and English in four successive wars called the Intercolonial Wars in Quebec and the [French and Indian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_and_Indian_Wars) by Americans.[^cite_note-48] The first three were [King William's War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_William's_War) (1688–1697), [Queen Anne's War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Anne's_War) (1702–1713), and [King George's War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_George's_War) (1744–1748). In 1713, following the [Peace of Utrecht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_of_Utrecht), the [Duke of Orléans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippe_II,_Duke_of_Orl%C3%A9ans) ceded Acadia and [Plaisance Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Placentia_Bay) to Great Britain, but retained [Île Saint-Jean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isle_Saint-Jean), and [Île-Royale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%8Ele-Royale_\(New_France\)) where the [Fortress of Louisbourg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortress_of_Louisbourg) was later erected. These losses were significant since Plaisance Bay was the primary communication route to France, and Acadia contained 5,000 French [Acadians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acadian_people).[^cite_note-49][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECharpentierDurocherLavilleLinteau1985115-50] In the [siege of Louisbourg (1745)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Louisbourg_\(1745\)), the British were victorious, but returned the city to France after war concessions.[^cite_note-51]



Montcalm leading his troops into battle. Watercolour by [Charles William Jefferys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_William_Jefferys).

The last of the four wars was the [Seven Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Years'_War) ("The War of the [Conquest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conquest_of_New_France)" in Quebec) and lasted from 1754 to 1763.[^cite_note-52][^cite_note-53] In 1754, tensions escalated for control of the [Ohio Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio_Valley), as authorities in New France became more aggressive in efforts to expel British traders and colonists.[^cite_note-54] In 1754, [George Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Washington) launched a surprise attack on a group of sleeping Canadien soldiers, known as the [Battle of Jumonville Glen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Jumonville_Glen), the war's first battle. In 1755, Governor [Charles Lawrence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Lawrence_\(British_Army_officer\)) and Officer [Robert Monckton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Monckton) ordered the forceful [expulsion of the Acadians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expulsion_of_the_Acadians). In 1758, on Île-Royale, British General [James Wolfe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Wolfe) [besieged and captured](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Louisbourg_\(1758\)) the Fortress of Louisbourg.[^cite_note-55] This allowed him to control access to the [Gulf of St. Lawrence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_St._Lawrence) through the [Cabot Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabot_Strait). In 1759, he besieged Quebec for three months from [Île d'Orléans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%8Ele_d'Orl%C3%A9ans).[^cite_note-56] Then, Wolfe stormed Quebec and fought [Montcalm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis-Joseph_de_Montcalm) for control of the city in the [Battle of the Plains of Abraham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Plains_of_Abraham). After a British victory, the [king's lieutenant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steward_\(office\)) and [Lord of Ramezay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Baptiste_Nicolas_Roch_de_Ramezay) concluded the [Articles of Capitulation of Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Articles_of_Capitulation_of_Quebec). During the spring of 1760, the [Chevalier de Lévis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Gaston_de_L%C3%A9vis) besieged Quebec City and forced the British to entrench themselves during the [Battle of Sainte-Foy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Sainte-Foy). However, loss of French vessels sent to resupply New France after the fall of Quebec City during the [Battle of Restigouche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Restigouche) marked the end of France's efforts to retake the colony. [Governor Pierre de Rigaud, marquis de Vaudreuil-Cavagnial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_de_Rigaud,_marquis_de_Vaudreuil-Cavagnial) signed the [Articles of Capitulation of Montreal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Articles_of_Capitulation_of_Montreal) on September 8, 1760.

While awaiting the results of the Seven Years' War in Europe, New France was put under a [British military regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_military_regime_in_New_France) led by Governor [James Murray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Murray_\(British_Army_officer,_born_1721\)).[^cite_note-britannica_murray-57] In 1762, Commander [Jeffery Amherst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffery_Amherst) ended French colonial presence in Newfoundland at the [Battle of Signal Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Signal_Hill). France secretly ceded the western part of Louisiana and the [Mississippi River Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_River_Delta) to Spain via the 1762 [Treaty of Fontainebleau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Fontainebleau_\(1762\)). On February 10, 1763, the [Treaty of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_\(1763\)) concluded the war. France ceded its North American possessions to Britain.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHunter1999505–506-58] Thus, France ended New France and abandoned the remaining 60,000 Canadiens, who sided with the Catholic clergy in refusing to swear fealty to the British Crown.[^cite_note-59] The rupture from France provoked a transformation within the [descendants of the Canadiens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Canadians) that eventually resulted in the birth of a new nation.[^cite_note-60]

### British North America (1763–1867)

Main articles: [Province of Quebec (1763–1791)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Quebec_\(1763%E2%80%931791\)), [Lower Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Canada), and [Canada East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_East)



The [Province of Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Quebec_\(1763-1791\)) in 1774

In 1763, the [Royal Proclamation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Proclamation_of_1763) established a constitution for the newly acquired territory.[^cite_note-61] The Canadiens became part of the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_British_Empire) and circumscribed to a region of the [St. Lawrence Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Lawrence_River) and [Anticosti Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anticosti_Island) called the [Province of Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Quebec_\(1763%E2%80%931791\)). With unrest growing to the south, the British were worried that the Canadiens might support the unrest. To secure their allegiance, Governor [James Murray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Murray_\(Quebec_governor\)) and later Governor [Guy Carleton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guy_Carleton,_1st_Baron_Dorchester) promoted the need for accommodations, resulting in the [Quebec Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_Act)[^cite_note-62] of 1774. This act allowed Canadiens to regain their [civil customs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_in_Quebec), return to the seigneural system, regain other rights including use of French, and reappropriate their old territories: Labrador, the Great Lakes, the Ohio Valley, [Illinois Country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_Country) and the [Indian Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Territory).[^cite_note-Geographers19682-63]

As early as 1774, the [Continental Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Congress) of the [Thirteen Colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteen_Colonies) attempted to rally the Canadiens to its cause. However, [its military troops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Army) failed to defeat the British counteroffensive during its [Invasion of Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Quebec_\(1775\)) in 1775. Most Canadiens remained neutral, though some regiments allied with the Americans in the [Saratoga campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saratoga_campaign) of 1777. When the British recognized the independence of the rebel colonies via the [Treaty of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_\(1783\)) of 1783, it ceded Illinois and the Ohio Valley to the newly formed United States and denoted the 45th parallel as its border, drastically shrinking Quebec.

Some [United Empire Loyalists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Empire_Loyalists) from the US migrated to Quebec.[^cite_note-64] Dissatisfied with the legal rights under the French seigneurial regime then followed in Quebec, and wanting to use the more familiar British legal system, the Loyalists protested to British authorities until the [Constitutional Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Act_1791) of 1791 was enacted, dividing Quebec into two colonies separated by the [Ottawa River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottawa_River): [Upper Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Canada) to the west (predominantly Anglo-Protestant) and [Lower Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Canada) to the east (Franco-Catholic). Lower Canada's lands consisted of the coasts of the Saint Lawrence River, Labrador and Anticosti Island, with the territory extending north to [Rupert's Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupert's_Land), and south, east and west to the borders with the US, New Brunswick, and Upper Canada. The creation of Upper and Lower Canada allowed Loyalists to live under British laws and institutions, while Canadiens maintained French civil law and Catholic religion. Governor Haldimand drew Loyalists away from Quebec City and Montreal by offering free land on the north shore of Lake Ontario to anyone willing to swear allegiance to George III. During the [War of 1812](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_1812), [Charles-Michel de Salaberry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles-Michel_de_Salaberry) became a hero by leading Canadian troops to victory at the [Battle of the Chateauguay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Chateauguay). This loss caused the Americans to abandon the Saint Lawrence Campaign, their strategic effort to conquer Canada.



The [Battle of Saint-Eustache](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Saint-Eustache) was the final battle of the Lower Canada Rebellion.

[^cite_note-rebellion-65]

Gradually, the [Legislative Assembly of Lower Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_Assembly_of_Lower_Canada) came into conflict with the superior authority of [the Crown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Crown) and [its appointed representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_Council_of_Lower_Canada). Starting in 1791, the government of Lower Canada was criticized and contested by [Parti canadien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parti_canadien). In 1834, the Parti canadien presented its [92 resolutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ninety-Two_Resolutions), political demands that expressed loss of confidence in the British monarchy. Discontentment intensified throughout the [public meetings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_the_public_meetings_held_in_Lower_Canada_between_May_and_November_1837) of 1837, and the [Lower Canada Rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Canada_Rebellion) began in 1837.[^cite_note-66] In 1837, [Louis-Joseph Papineau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis-Joseph_Papineau) and [Robert Nelson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Nelson_\(insurrectionist\)) led residents of Lower Canada to form the [Patriotes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriotes), an armed group. [They declared independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_Independence_of_Lower_Canada) in 1838, guaranteeing rights and equality for all citizens without discrimination.[^cite_note-67] Their actions triggered rebellions in both Lower and [Upper Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Canada_Rebellion). The Patriotes were victorious in their first battle, the [Battle of Saint-Denis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Saint-Denis_\(1837\)). However, they were disorganized and badly equipped, leading to their loss against the British army in the [Battle of Saint-Charles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Saint-Charles), and defeat in the [Battle of Saint-Eustache](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Saint-Eustache).[^cite_note-rebellion-65]

In response to the rebellions, [Lord Durham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Lambton,_1st_Earl_of_Durham) was asked to undertake a study and prepare a [report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Report_on_the_Affairs_of_British_North_America) to Parliament.[^cite_note-durham2-68] Durham recommended that Canadiens be [culturally assimilated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_assimilation), with English as their only official language. To do this, the British passed the *[Act of Union 1840](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Act_of_Union_1840)*, which merged Upper Canada and Lower Canada into a single colony: the [Province of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Canada). Lower Canada became the [francophone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francophone) and densely populated [Canada East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_East), and Upper Canada became the anglophone and sparsely populated [Canada West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_West). This union, unsurprisingly, was the main source of political instability until 1867. Despite their population gap, Canada East and Canada West obtained an identical number of seats in the [Legislative Assembly of the Province of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_Assembly_of_the_Province_of_Canada), which created representation problems. Initially, equal legislative representation underrepresented Canada East due to its larger population. Heavy immigration from the [British Isles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Isles) to Canada West later reversed this demographic balance, leaving Canada West underrepresented under the same framework. The representation issues were called into question by debates on [representation by population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Representation_\(politics\)). Around this period, the British population appropriated the term *Canadian* to refer to themselves. The French population, who had thus far been "the Canadians", began to be identified with their ethnic community under the name "[French Canadian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Canadian)" as they were a "French of Canada".[^cite_note-69]

Access to new lands was problematic because they were monopolized by the [Château Clique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ch%C3%A2teau_Clique), causing an exodus of Canadiens towards [New England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_England) for the next hundred years. This phenomenon is known as the [Grande Hémorragie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grande_H%C3%A9morragie) and threatened the survival of the Canadien nation. The massive British immigration ordered from London that followed the failed rebellion compounded this. To combat it, the Church adopted the [revenge of the cradle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Revanche_des_berceaux) policy. In 1844, the capital of the Province of Canada was moved from [Kingston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingston,_Ontario) to Montreal.[^cite_note-70] During Ireland's [Great Potato Famine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Potato_Famine) (1845–1852), nearly 100,000 Irish refugees passed through [Grosse Isle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grosse_Isle)'s quarantine station, with many settling in Quebec and integrating into French-Canadian society.[^cite_note-71][^cite_note-72]

Political unrest came to a head in 1849, when English Canadian rioters [set fire to the Parliament Building in Montreal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burning_of_the_Parliament_Buildings_in_Montreal) following the enactment of the *[Rebellion Losses Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebellion_Losses_Bill)*, a law that compensated French Canadians whose properties were destroyed during the rebellions of 1837–1838.[^cite_note-73] This bill, resulting from the [Baldwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Baldwin)-[La Fontaine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis-Hippolyte_Lafontaine) coalition and [Lord Elgin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Bruce,_7th_Earl_of_Elgin)'s advice, was important as it established the notion of [responsible government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Responsible_government).[^cite_note-74] In 1854, the seigneurial system was abolished, the [Grand Trunk Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Trunk_Railway) was built, and the [Canadian–American Reciprocity Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian%E2%80%93American_Reciprocity_Treaty) was implemented. In 1866, the *[Civil Code of Lower Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Code_of_Lower_Canada)* was adopted.[^cite_note-75][^cite_note-76][^cite_note-77]

### Traditional Quebec (1867–1960)



[George-Étienne Cartier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George-%C3%89tienne_Cartier), co-premier from [Canada East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada_East) and a Father of Confederation

In 1864, negotiations began for [Canadian Confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Confederation) between the Province of Canada, New Brunswick and Nova Scotia at the [Charlottetown Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlottetown_Conference) and [Quebec Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_Conference,_1864).

After having fought as a Patriote, [George-Étienne Cartier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George-%C3%89tienne_Cartier) entered politics in the Province of Canada, becoming one of the co-premiers and advocate for the union of the British North American provinces. He became a leading figure at the Quebec Conference, which produced the [Quebec Resolutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_Resolutions), the foundation for Canadian Confederation.[^cite_note-78] Recognized as a [Father of Confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Father_of_Confederation), he successfully argued for the establishment of the province of Quebec, initially composed of the historic heart of the territory of the French Canadian nation.

Following the [London Conference of 1866](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Conference_of_1866), the Quebec Resolutions were implemented as the *[British North America Act, 1867](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_North_America_Act,_1867)*, and brought into force on July 1, 1867, creating the nation of Canada, composed of four provinces: New Brunswick, Nova Scotia, Ontario and Quebec. These last two came from splitting the Province of Canada, and used the old borders of Lower Canada for Quebec, and Upper Canada for Ontario. On July 15, 1867, [Pierre-Joseph-Olivier Chauveau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre-Joseph-Olivier_Chauveau) became Quebec's first premier.

Between the late 19th and the late 20th century, Montreal was Canada's most populous city, including its economic and cultural centres. It was, as such, often among the first to adopt new technologies. It launched Canada's first public transit system in 1861 with horse-drawn streetcars,[^cite_note-79] started a telephone service in 1878,[^cite_note-80] and began generating electricity in 1885.[^cite_note-81]

The new Dominion quickly became interested in [expansionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expansionism), especially westward, purchasing [Rupert's Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupert's_Land) from the [Hudson's Bay Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson's_Bay_Company) in 1870. In 1885, it fought against the francophone Métis in the [North-West Rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North-West_Rebellion), and did not grant [clemency](2176497.md#Canada) to [Louis Riel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Riel), their leader, after he was sentenced to death. This caused several Quebec liberal and conservative MLAs to form the [Parti National](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parti_National) out of anger.[^cite_note-82] This, in combination with the [Manitoba Schools Question](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manitoba_Schools_Question), also helped turn the rights of French Canadians into an important concern. Gradually gaining in popularity, clerico-nationalists – who promoted the Triple Ideal of Catholicism, French, and rural life, alongside other traditional values (e.g., traditional gender roles, resistance to cultural assimilation, anti-[progressivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressivism), and hierarchy) – wielded significant influence until the 1960s.[^cite_note-83]

Montreal continued its expansions into new advances by introducing streetcars in 1892[^cite_note-84] and seeing bikes and automobiles populate its roads by the 1890s and 1900s respectively.[^cite_note-85] The Canadian Parliament, meanwhile, expanded Quebec in 1898 by enacting the *[Quebec Boundary Extension Act, 1898](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_Boundary_Extension_Act,_1898)*, which added part of Rupert's Land.[^cite_note-86]

Under the aegis of the Catholic Church and the political action of [Henri Bourassa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henri_Bourassa), symbols of French Canadian national pride were developed, like the [Flag of Carillon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Carillon), and "[O Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O_Canada)" – a patriotic song composed for [Saint-Jean-Baptiste Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Jean-Baptiste_Day). Many organizations to consecrate the affirmation of the French-Canadian people, including the [caisses populaires Desjardins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caisses_populaires_Desjardins) in 1900, the [Club de hockey Canadien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Club_de_hockey_Canadien) in 1909, *[Le Devoir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Devoir)* in 1910, the [Congress on the French language in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_on_the_French_language_in_Canada) in 1912, and *[L'Action nationale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L'Action_nationale)* in 1917. In 1909, the Quebec government passed a law obligating wood and pulp to be transformed in Quebec, which helped slow the Grande Hémorragie by allowing Quebec to export its finished products to the US instead of its labourers.[^cite_note-87] In 1910, [Armand Lavergne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armand_Lavergne) passed the [Lavergne Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lavergne_Law), the first language legislation in Quebec, which required the use of French alongside English on tickets, documents, bills and contracts issued by transportation and public utility companies. At this time, companies rarely recognized the majority language of Quebec.[^cite_note-88] This movement may be why Ontario's [Regulation 17](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regulation_17) (1912–1927) was fought until its repeal.

In 1912, the Canadian Parliament enacted the *[Quebec Boundaries Extension Act, 1912](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_Boundaries_Extension_Act,_1912)*, which gave Quebec its final extension: another part of Rupert's Land called the [District of Ungava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Ungava).[^cite_note-Morantz20022-89] Quebec's borders then reached the [Hudson Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_Strait) and vaguely interlapped with [Labrador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_of_Newfoundland)'s.

When the [First World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_World_War) broke out in 1914, Canada was involved and many English Canadians volunteered. However, because they did not feel the same connection to the British Empire and absent a direct threat to Canada, most French Canadians saw no reason to fight. By late 1916, casualties and waning numbers of volunteers were beginning to cause reinforcement problems. Although almost every French-speaking MP opposed [conscription](5735.md), while almost all English-speaking MPs supported it, the *[Military Service Act, 1917](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_Service_Act,_1917)* became law on August 29, 1917.[^cite_note-Conscription_au_Canada-90] French Canadians protested, creating the [Conscription Crisis of 1917](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscription_Crisis_of_1917), which led to the [Quebec riot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_riot?action=edit\&redlink=1).[^cite_note-91]

In 1919, the [prohibition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prohibition) of spirits was enacted following a [provincial referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1919_Quebec_prohibition_referendum).[^cite_note-92] In 1920, Montreal hosted Canada's first public radio broadcast.[^cite_note-93] However, in 1921 prohibition was abolished by the *Alcoholic Beverages Act,* which created the [SAQ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soci%C3%A9t%C3%A9_des_alcools_du_Qu%C3%A9bec) and allowed the government to regulate the sale of alcohol.[^cite_note-94] Quebec had the shortest and weakest prohibition in North America, as well as profiting from sale to tourists.[^cite_note-95] Since the border between Canada and Labrador had never been formalized, in 1927, the British [Judicial Committee of the Privy Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_Committee_of_the_Privy_Council) gathered to draw one. However, the Quebec government did not recognize the ruling, resulting in a [continuing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_between_Quebec_and_Newfoundland_and_Labrador) [boundary dispute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labrador_boundary_dispute).

In 1931, the [Statute of Westminster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statute_of_Westminster_1931) was enacted, which confirmed the autonomy of the [Dominions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion) – including Canada and its provinces – from the UK, as well as their free association in the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations).[^cite_note-96] In the 1930s, Quebec's economy was affected by the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression) because of reduced US demand for Quebec products. Between 1929 and 1932, the unemployment rate tripled, from 8% to 26%. In an attempt to remedy this, the Quebec government enacted infrastructure projects, campaigns to colonize distant regions, financial assistance to farmers, and the *secours directs* – the ancestor to Canada's [Employment Insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Employment_Insurance).[^cite_note-97] The Depression ended the Grande Hémorragie.



[Maurice Duplessis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_Duplessis), premier of Quebec from 1936 to 1939 and during the *[Grande Noirceur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grande_Noirceur)*. He passed in 1959.

French Canadians remained opposed to conscription during the [Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War). When Canada declared war in September 1939, the federal government pledged not to conscript soldiers for overseas service. As the war went on, more and more English Canadians voiced support for conscription, despite opposition from French Canada. Following a 1942 poll that showed 73% of Quebec's residents were *against* conscription, while 80% or more were *for* conscription in every other province, the federal government passed Bill 80, which allowed conscription for overseas service. In the [Conscription Crisis of 1944](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscription_Crisis_of_1944) the [Bloc Populaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloc_Populaire) emerged to fight conscription.[^cite_note-Conscription_au_Canada-90] The stark differences between the values of French and English Canada popularized the expression the "[Two Solitudes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Two_Solitudes_\(Canadian_society\))".

In the wake of the conscription crisis, [Maurice Duplessis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_Duplessis) of the [Union Nationale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Nationale_\(Quebec\)) again rose to power. His government emphasized clerico-nationalist values and implemented conservative policies (the [Grande Noirceur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grande_Noirceur)). These included defending [provincial autonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomism_in_Quebec), promoting Quebec's Catholic and francophone heritage, and favouring [laissez-faire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laissez-faire) [capitalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitalism) over the emerging [welfare state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare_state).[^cite_note-98] However, with changes such as the appearance of television, [baby boom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mid-20th_century_baby_boom), [workers' conflicts](155134.md), electrification of the countryside, emergence of a [middle class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_class), [rural exodus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rural_exodus) and [urbanization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbanization), expansion of universities and bureaucracies, creation of [motorways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autoroutes_of_Quebec), and renaissance of literature and poetry, French Canadian society began to develop new ideologies and aspirations.

### Modern Quebec (1960–present)



"*Maîtres chez nous*" was the electoral slogan of the [Liberal Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_Liberal_Party) during the [1962 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1962_Quebec_general_election).

The [Quiet Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quiet_Revolution) was an intense period of modernization, secularization and social reform, where French Canadians expressed their dissatisfaction with their inferior socioeconomic position, and the cultural assimilation of francophone minorities in English-majority provinces. It resulted in the formation of the modern [Québécois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qu%C3%A9b%C3%A9cois_people) identity and [Quebec nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_nationalism).[^cite_note-99][^cite_note-short_history2-100] In 1960, [Jean Lesage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Lesage)'s Liberal Party was brought to power with a two-seat majority, having campaigned with the slogan "It's time for things to change".

This government fundamentally restructured Quebec's institutions, creating a modern welfare state through new ministries for education, social affairs, and economic development. It created the [CDPQ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caisse_de_d%C3%A9p%C3%B4t_et_placement_du_Qu%C3%A9bec), [Ministry of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Education_and_Higher_Education_\(Quebec\)), [OQLF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_qu%C3%A9b%C3%A9cois_de_la_langue_fran%C3%A7aise), Régie des rentes and [Société générale de financement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Investissement_Qu%C3%A9bec), and modernized the Labour Code and [Ministry of Social Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Health_and_Social_Services_\(Quebec\)). In 1962, the government dismantled the financial syndicates of Montreal's [Saint Jacques Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Jacques_Street) to weaken the grip of the English-Canadian traditional economic elites. Also in 1962, Natural Resources Minister [René Lévesque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_L%C3%A9vesque) led the nationalization of Quebec's private electricity companies to [create a unified Hydro-Québec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Hydro-Qu%C3%A9bec#1963:_Second_stage_of_state_control). This massive project was estimated at over \$600 million for the acquisition of eleven companies.[^cite_note-101][^cite_note-102]

The Quiet Revolution was particularly characterized by the 1962 Liberal Party's slogan "Masters in our own house", which, to the Anglo-American conglomerates that dominated the economy and natural resources, announced a collective will for freedom of the French-Canadian people.[^cite_note-103] As a result of confrontations between the lower [clergy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clergy) and the [laity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laity), state institutions began to deliver services without the church, and many parts of civil society became more secular. In 1965, the [Royal Commission on Bilingualism and Biculturalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Commission_on_Bilingualism_and_Biculturalism)[^cite_note-104] wrote a preliminary report underlining Quebec's distinct character, and promoted open federalism, a political attitude guaranteeing Quebec a minimum amount of autonomy.[^cite_note-105][^cite_note-106] To favour Quebec during its Quiet Revolution, Prime Minister [Lester B. Pearson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lester_B._Pearson) adopted a policy of open federalism.[^cite_note-107][^cite_note-108] In 1966, the Union Nationale was re-elected and continued major reforms.[^cite_note-109]



[Charles De Gaulle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle), (1890–1970), on the occasion of [Expo 1967](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expo_67), [Chemin du Roy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemin_du_Roy), [Sainte-Anne-de-la-Pérade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sainte-Anne-de-la-P%C3%A9rade)

In 1967, René Lévesque introduced the concept of *sovereignty-association* in his manifesto [Option Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/An_Option_for_Quebec), proposing political independence with economic partnership including a common currency, free trade, and joint institutions. It sparked a constitutional debate on the province's political future that set [federalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalism_in_Quebec) and [sovereignist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_sovereignty_movement) doctrines against each other. The meetings of the [Estates General of French Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estates_General_of_French_Canada) in 1967 marked a tipping point where relations between Quebec and other francophones of Canada ruptured. This deeply affected both parties by breaking the pan-Canadian French-Canadian identity that had existed before then into: Quebec nationalism, and minority francophone groups elsewhere.[^cite_note-110] Also in 1967, President of France [Charles de Gaulle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle) visited Quebec to attend [Expo 67](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expo_67). There, he addressed a crowd of more than 100,000, ending with the exclamation: "Long live free Quebec". This declaration had a profound effect by bolstering the burgeoning modern Quebec sovereignty movement and sparking a diplomatic crisis between France and Canada. Following this, various civilian groups developed, sometimes confronting public authority, for example in the [October Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_Crisis) of 1970.[^cite_note-Tetley2006AppendixD2-111]

In 1968, [class conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class_conflict) and changes in mindset intensified.[^cite_note-112] Quebec artists started celebrating their distinct identity: [Michel Tremblay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Tremblay)'s 1968 play *Les Belles-sœurs* legitimized [joual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joual) (working-class Quebec French) as a literary language,[^cite_note-113] singer-songwriters like [Félix Leclerc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%A9lix_Leclerc) and [Gilles Vigneault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilles_Vigneault) started a new style of Quebec popular music,[^cite_note-114] and many [local films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Quebec) debuted.[^cite_note-115] In 1969, the federal [Official Languages Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_Languages_Act_\(Canada\)) was passed to introduce a linguistic context conducive to Quebec's development.[^cite_note-116][^cite_note-117] In 1973, the liberal government of [Robert Bourassa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Bourassa) initiated the [James Bay Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Bay_Project) on [La Grande River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Grande_River). In 1974, it enacted the [Official Language Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_Language_Act_\(Quebec\)), which made French the official language of Quebec. In 1975, it established the [Charter of Human Rights and Freedoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_Charter_of_Human_Rights_and_Freedoms) and the [James Bay and Northern Quebec Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Bay_and_Northern_Quebec_Agreement).



[René Lévesque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_L%C3%A9vesque) (1922–1987), one of the architects of the Quiet Revolution, and the Premier of Quebec's first modern sovereignist government

Quebec's first modern sovereignist government, led by [René Lévesque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_L%C3%A9vesque), materialized when [Parti Québécois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parti_Qu%C3%A9b%C3%A9cois) came to power in [1976](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1976_Quebec_general_election).[^cite_note-118] The [Charter of the French Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charter_of_the_French_Language) came into force the following year, which increased the use of French. Between 1966 and 1969, the Estates General of French Canada confirmed the state of Quebec to be the [nation's fundamental political milieu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation-state) and for it to have the right to [self-determination](29269.md).[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-120]

In the [1980 referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Quebec_referendum) on sovereignty-association, 40% were for and 60% were against.[^cite_note-121] After the referendum, Lévesque went back to Ottawa to continue negotiating constitutional changes. On November 4, 1981, the [Kitchen Accord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kitchen_Accord) took place. Delegations from the other nine provinces and the federal government reached an agreement in the absence of Quebec's delegation, which had left for the night.[^cite_note-122] Because of this, the [National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Assembly_of_Quebec) refused to recognize the new [Constitution Act, 1982](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_Act,_1982), which patriated the Canadian constitution and made modifications to it.[^cite_note-123] The 1982 amendments apply to Quebec, despite Quebec never having consented to it.[^cite_note-124]

Between 1982 and 1992, the Quebec government's attitude changed to prioritize reforming the federation. Attempts at constitutional amendments by the [Mulroney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Mulroney) and Bourassa governments ended in failure with the [Meech Lake Accord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meech_Lake_Accord) of 1987 and the [Charlottetown Accord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlottetown_Accord) of 1992, resulting in the creation of the [Bloc Québécois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloc_Qu%C3%A9b%C3%A9cois).[^cite_note-125][^cite_note-126] The failures also led to the re-election of the Parti Québécois in 1994, and the return to power of [Jacques Parizeau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Parizeau), who had promised to hold a sovereignty referendum within a year of election. In 1995, Parizeau called a [referendum on Quebec's independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995_Quebec_referendum) from Canada. This consultation ended in a close outcome: 50.6% "no" and 49.4% "yes" (notably, over 60% of francophones voted "yes" and over 90% of anglophones voted "no").[^cite_note-1995_facts2-127][^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129]

In 1996, the federal government launched the Sponsorship Program to increase federal visibility in Quebec. In 2000, following the [Supreme Court of Canada](123468.md)'s decision on the [Reference Re Secession of Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reference_Re_Secession_of_Quebec), the Parliament of Canada passed a legal framework, called the [Clarity Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarity_Act), within which governments would act in another referendum. In 2002, the [Gomery commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gomery_commission) and media revealed the Sponsorship Program, in which \$539,000 was illegally spent and where well-connected agencies received millions for minimal work. This [scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sponsorship_scandal) contributed to the Liberals' defeat in the [2006 federal election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Canadian_federal_election).[^cite_note-130] On October 30, 2003, the National Assembly voted unanimously to affirm "that the people of Québec form a nation".[^cite_note-131] On November 27, 2006, the House of Commons followed with a [symbolic motion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qu%C3%A9b%C3%A9cois_nation_motion) declaring "that this House recognize that the Québécois form a nation within a united Canada."[^cite_note-132]

In 2007, the Parti Québécois was pushed back to official opposition in the National Assembly, with the Liberal party leading. During the [2011 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Canadian_federal_election), Quebec voters rejected the Bloc Québécois in favour of the previously minor [New Democratic Party (NDP)](19283982.md).[^cite_note-133] In 2012, the Liberal party, led by [Jean Charest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Charest), announced an increase in student tuition fees. This spawned months-long protests involving over 300,000 students known as the [Maple Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Quebec_student_protests), ultimately leading to a rollback of the increases.[^cite_note-134] Also partially as a result, the Liberal party fell out of favour, letting the Parti Québécois regain power in 2012 and its leader, [Pauline Marois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pauline_Marois), to become the first female premier of Quebec.[^cite_note-135] The Liberal Party of Quebec then returned to power in 2014.[^cite_note-136] Then, in 2018, the [Coalition Avenir Québec (CAQ)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_Avenir_Qu%C3%A9bec) won the [provincial general elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Quebec_general_election).[^cite_note-137]

Between 2020 and 2021, Quebec took measures against the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Quebec).[^cite_note-138] In 2022, the CAQ, led by Quebec's premier [François Legault](6899647.md), increased its parliamentary majority in the [provincial general elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Quebec_general_election).[^cite_note-139] In 2025, following the [implementation of tariffs](78448911.md) and [aggressive rhetoric](79100181.md) by the US, Quebecers decreased their travel to the US,[^cite_note-140] banned the sale of American alcohol,[^cite_note-141] and slightly reduced personal purchases of US items.[^cite_note-142]

## Geography

This section is an excerpt from [Geography of Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Quebec).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Geography_of_Quebec\&action=edit)]



The Quebec territory.



Approximate areas of [ecoregions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecoregion)&#x20;

&#x20; 1. Middle Arctic Tundra

&#x20; 2. Low Arctic Tundra

&#x20; 3. Torngat Mountain Tundra

&#x20; 4. Eastern Canadian Shield Taiga

&#x20; 5. Southern Hudson Bay Taiga

&#x20; 6. Central Canadian Shield Forests

&#x20; 7. Eastern Canadian Forests

&#x20; 8. Eastern Forest/Boreal Transition

&#x20; 9. Eastern Great Lakes Lowland Forests

&#x20; 10. New England/Acadian Forests

&#x20; 11. Gulf of St. Lawrence Lowland Forests

Located in the [eastern part](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Canada) of Canada, Quebec occupies a territory nearly three times the size of France or Texas.[^cite_note-143] It is much closer to the size of [Alaska](624.md), covering 1.5 million square kilometres (0.58 million square miles), with borders more than 12,000 km (7,500 mi) long.[^cite_note-Geography_of_Quebec_Geography_of_Quebec_territory-144][^cite_note-Geography_of_Quebec_statcan-area-145][^cite_note-Geography_of_Quebec_qcborders-146] It is the [largest province in Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Canadian_provinces_and_territories_by_area) and, by total area, the [tenth-largest country subdivision in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_country_subdivisions_by_area).[^cite_note-Geography_of_Quebec_statcan-area-145]

Most of Quebec is sparsely populated.[^cite_note-147] Its most populous region is the [Great Lakes–St. Lawrence Lowlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes%E2%80%93St._Lawrence_Lowlands).[^cite_note-Geography_of_Quebec_Elson2-148][^cite_note-Geography_of_Quebec_NAC2-149]

The combination of rich soils and the lowlands' relatively warm climate makes this valley its most prolific agricultural area.[^cite_note-Geography_of_Quebec_Elson2-148][^cite_note-Geography_of_Quebec_NAC2-149] The rural landscape is divided into narrow rectangular tracts of land that extend from the river and date to the [seigneurial system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seigneurial_system_of_New_France).

Quebec's [topography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topography) is different from one region to another due to the varying composition of the ground, the climate, and the proximity to water. The [Great Lakes–St. Lawrence Lowlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Lakes%E2%80%93St._Lawrence_Lowlands) and the [Appalachians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appalachian_Mountains) are the two main topographic regions in southern Quebec, while the [Canadian Shield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Shield) occupies most of central and northern Quebec.[^cite_note-Geography_of_Quebec_Elson2-148][^cite_note-Geography_of_Quebec_naturalhistory2-150] 95% of the total,[^cite_note-Geography_of_Quebec_Elson2-148] including the [Labrador Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labrador_Peninsula), lies within the [Canadian Shield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian_Shield).[^cite_note-Geography_of_Quebec_naturalhistory2-150] It is generally flat and exposed mountainous terrain interspersed with higher points such as the [Laurentian Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurentian_Mountains) in southern Quebec, the [Otish Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Otish_Mountains) in central Quebec and the [Torngat Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torngat_Mountains) near [Ungava Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ungava_Bay). While low and medium altitude peaks extend from western Quebec to the far north, high altitude mountains appear in the [Capitale-Nationale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitale-Nationale) region to the east. Quebec's highest point at 1,652 metres (5,420 ft) is Mont d'Iberville, known in English as [Mount Caubvick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Caubvick), in the northeastern part of the province in the [Torngat Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torngat_Mountains).[^cite_note-151] In the Labrador Peninsula portion of the Shield, the far northern region of [Nunavik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nunavik) includes the Ungava Peninsula and consists of flat Arctic [tundra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tundra) inhabited mostly by Inuit.[^cite_note-Geography_of_Quebec_naturalhistory2-150][^cite_note-Geography_of_Quebec_Ecological_classification_of_Quebec_territory-152]

Further south is the [Eastern Canadian Shield taiga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Canadian_Shield_taiga) ecoregion and the [Central Canadian Shield forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Canadian_Shield_forests). The [Appalachian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaudi%C3%A8re-Appalaches) region has a narrow strip of ancient mountains along the southeastern border of Quebec.[^cite_note-Geography_of_Quebec_Ecological_classification_of_Quebec_territory-152]



Michel's falls on [Ashuapmushuan River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashuapmushuan_River) in [Saint-Félicien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-F%C3%A9licien,_Qu%C3%A9bec), [Saguenay–Lac-Saint-Jean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saguenay%E2%80%93Lac-Saint-Jean)

Quebec has one of the world's largest reserves of fresh water,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMinistry_of_Environment_of_Quebec20025-153] occupying 12% of its surface[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBabin198639-154] and representing 3% of the world's [renewable fresh water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_freshwater_resources).[^cite_note-155] More than half a million lakes and 4,500 rivers[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMinistry_of_Environment_of_Quebec20025-153] empty into the [Atlantic Ocean](698.md), through the [Gulf of Saint Lawrence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Saint_Lawrence) and the [Arctic Ocean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic_Ocean), by [James](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Bay), [Hudson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_Bay), and Ungava bays. The largest inland body of water is the [Caniapiscau Reservoir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caniapiscau_Reservoir); [Lake Mistassini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Mistassini) is the largest natural lake.[^cite_note-Geography_of_Quebec_CTQ-resC2-156] The [Saint Lawrence River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Lawrence_River) has some of the world's largest sustaining inland Atlantic ports. Since 1959, the [Saint Lawrence Seaway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Lawrence_Seaway) has provided a navigable link between the Atlantic Ocean and the Great Lakes.

The [public lands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_lands) of Quebec cover approximately 92% of its territory, including almost all of the water bodies. [Protected areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protected_areas_of_Quebec) can be classified into about twenty different legal designations (ex. exceptional forest ecosystem, protected marine environment, [national park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Parks_of_Quebec), [biodiversity reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity_reserves_of_Quebec), wildlife reserve, [zone d'exploitation contrôlée](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zone_d'exploitation_contr%C3%B4l%C3%A9e) (ZEC), etc.).[^cite_note-Geography_of_Quebec_definition2-157] More than 2,500 sites in Quebec today are protected areas.[^cite_note-158] As of 2013, protected areas comprise 9.14% of Quebec's territory.[^cite_note-Geography_of_Quebec_register2-159]

The ecological classification of Quebec territory established by the [Ministry of Forests, Wildlife and Parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Energy_and_Natural_Resources_\(Quebec\)) 2021, is presented in nine levels, it includes the diversity of terrestrial [ecosystems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecosystem) throughout Quebec while taking into account both the characteristics of the [vegetation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vegetation) (physiognomy, structure and composition) and the physical environment (relief, [geology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geology), [geomorphology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geomorphology), [hydrography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrography)).[^cite_note-Geography_of_Quebec_Ecological_classification_of_Quebec_territory-152]

The territory of Quebec is rich in resources in its [coniferous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conifer) forests, lakes, and rivers—[pulp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wood_pulp) and paper, lumber, and [hydroelectricity](381399.md) are some of the province's most important industries.[^cite_note-Geography_of_Quebec_naturalhistory2-150][^cite_note-Geography_of_Quebec_domaine_forestier2-160][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMinistry_of_Environment_of_Quebec20025-153] The far north of the province, [Nunavik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nunavik), is [subarctic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subarctic) or [Arctic](36971.md) and is mostly inhabited by [Inuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inuit).[^cite_note-Geography_of_Quebec_naturalhistory2-150][^cite_note-Geography_of_Quebec_Ecological_classification_of_Quebec_territory-152]

## Government

Main article: [Government of Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Quebec)



The [Parliament Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_Building_\(Quebec\)) in Quebec City

Quebec is founded on the [Westminster system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westminster_system), and is both a [liberal democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_democracy) and a [constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy) with [parliamentary regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_system). The head of government in Quebec is the [premier](370751.md) (called *premier ministre* in French), who leads the largest party in the [unicameral](204304.md) National Assembly (*Assemblée Nationale*) from which the [Executive Council of Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Council_of_Quebec) is appointed. The [Conseil du trésor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secr%C3%A9tariat_du_Conseil_du_tr%C3%A9sor_\(Quebec\)) supports the ministers of the Executive Council in their function of stewardship of the state. The [lieutenant governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lieutenant_Governor_of_Quebec) represents the [King of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Canada).[^cite_note-161][^cite_note-162]

Quebec has 78 [members of Parliament](66805492.md) (MPs) in the [House of Commons of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_Canada).[^cite_note-163] They are elected in federal elections. At the level of the [Senate of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_Canada), Quebec is represented by 24 senators, which are appointed on the advice of the [prime minister of Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_minister_of_Canada).[^cite_note-164]

The Quebec government holds [administrative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administration_\(government\)) and [police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police) authority in its areas of [exclusive jurisdiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusive_jurisdiction). The Parliament of the [43rd legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/43rd_Quebec_Legislature) is made up of the following parties: [Coalition Avenir Québec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_Avenir_Qu%C3%A9bec) (CAQ), [Parti libéral du Québec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parti_lib%C3%A9ral_du_Qu%C3%A9bec) (PLQ), [Québec solidaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qu%C3%A9bec_solidaire) (QS) and [Parti Québécois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parti_Qu%C3%A9b%C3%A9cois) (PQ), as well as an [independent member](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Assembly_of_Quebec#Current_standings). There are [25 official political parties in Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_political_parties_in_Quebec).[^cite_note-165]

Quebec has a network of three offices for representing itself and defending its interests within Canada: one in Moncton for all provinces east, one in Toronto for all provinces west, and one in Ottawa for the federal government. These offices' mandate is to ensure an institutional presence of the Government of Quebec near other Canadian governments.[^cite_note-166][^cite_note-167]

### Subdivisions

Main article: [Administrative divisions of Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_Quebec)



The [seventeen administrative regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_Quebec) of Quebec

Quebec's territory is divided into 17 [administrative regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_Quebec) as follows:[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169]

1. [Bas-Saint-Laurent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bas-Saint-Laurent)

2. [Saguenay–Lac-Saint-Jean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saguenay%E2%80%93Lac-Saint-Jean)

3. [Capitale-Nationale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitale-Nationale)

4. [Mauricie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mauricie)

5. [Estrie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estrie)

6. [Montréal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_agglomeration_of_Montreal)

7. [Outaouais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outaouais)

8. [Abitibi-Témiscamingue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abitibi-T%C3%A9miscamingue)

9. [Côte-Nord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%B4te-Nord)

10. [Nord-du-Québec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nord-du-Qu%C3%A9bec)

11. [Gaspésie–Îles-de-la-Madeleine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gasp%C3%A9sie%E2%80%93%C3%8Eles-de-la-Madeleine)

12. [Chaudière-Appalaches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaudi%C3%A8re-Appalaches)

13. [Laval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laval,_Quebec)

14. [Lanaudière](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lanaudi%C3%A8re)

15. [Laurentides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurentides)

16. [Montérégie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mont%C3%A9r%C3%A9gie)

17. [Centre-du-Québec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre-du-Qu%C3%A9bec)

The province also has the following divisions:

- 4 territories ([Abitibi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abitibi_County,_Quebec), [Ashuanipi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashuanipi), [Mistassini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rivi%C3%A8re-Mistassini,_Quebec) and [Nunavik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nunavik)) which group together the lands that once formed the [District of Ungava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_of_Ungava)

- 36 [judicial districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_districts_of_Quebec)

- 73 *circonscriptions foncières*

- 125 [electoral districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_districts_of_Quebec?action=edit\&redlink=1)

  [^cite_note-170]

For municipal purposes, Quebec is composed of:

- 1,117 [local municipalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_Quebec) of various [types](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Types_of_municipalities_in_Quebec):&#x20;

  - 11 [agglomerations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_agglomerations_of_Quebec) (*agglomérations*) grouping 42 of these local municipalities

  - 45 [boroughs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_boroughs_in_Quebec) (*arrondissements*) within 8 of these local municipalities

- 89 [regional county municipalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_county_municipality) or RCMs (*municipalités régionales de comté, MRC*)

- 2 [metropolitan communities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Community_\(Quebec\)) (*communautés métropolitaines*)

- the regional [Kativik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kativik_Regional_Government) administration

- the [unorganised territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_unorganized_territories_in_Quebec)

  [^cite_note-171]

### Ministries and policies

Quebec's constitution is enshrined in a series of social and cultural traditions that are defined in a set of judicial judgments and legislative documents, including the *Loi sur l'Assemblée Nationale* ("Law on the National Assembly"), the *Loi sur l'éxecutif* ("Law on the Executive"), and the *Loi électorale du Québec* ("Electoral Law of Quebec").[^cite_note-172] Other notable examples include the [Charter of Human Rights and Freedoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_Charter_of_Human_Rights_and_Freedoms), the [Charter of the French language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charter_of_the_French_language), and the [Civil Code of Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Code_of_Quebec).[^cite_note-CCQ2-173]

Quebec's international policy is founded upon the [Gérin-Lajoie doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%A9rin-Lajoie_doctrine?action=edit\&redlink=1),[^cite_note-174] formulated in 1965. While Quebec's [Ministry of International Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_International_Relations_\(Quebec\)) coordinates international policy, [Quebec's general delegations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec_Government_Offices) are the main interlocutors in foreign countries. Quebec is the only Canadian province that has set up a ministry to exclusively embody the state's powers for international relations.[^cite_note-175]

Since 2006, Quebec has adopted a green plan to meet the objectives of the [Kyoto Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyoto_Protocol) regarding climate change.[^cite_note-176] The [Ministry of Sustainable Development, Environment, and Fight Against Climate Change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Sustainable_Development,_Environment,_and_Fight_Against_Climate_Change) (MELCC) is the primary entity responsible for the application of environmental policy. The [Société des établissements de plein air du Québec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soci%C3%A9t%C3%A9_des_%C3%A9tablissements_de_plein_air_du_Qu%C3%A9bec) (SEPAQ) is the main body responsible for the management of national parks and wildlife reserves.[^cite_note-177] Nearly 500,000 people took part in a climate protest on the streets of Montreal in 2019.[^cite_note-178]

Agriculture in Quebec has been subject to [agricultural zoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_zoning) regulations since 1978.[^cite_note-179] Faced with the problem of expanding [urban sprawl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_sprawl), agricultural zones were created to ensure the protection of fertile land, which make up 2% of Quebec's total area. [Quebec's forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quebec's_forests?action=edit\&redlink=1) are essentially public property. The calculation of annual cutting possibilities is the responsibility of the *Bureau du forestier en chef*.[^cite_note-180] The [Union des producteurs agricoles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_des_producteurs_agricoles) (UPA) seeks to protect the interests of its members, including forestry workers, and works jointly with the [Ministry of Agriculture, Fisheries and Food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Agriculture,_Fisheries_and_Food_\(Quebec\)) (MAPAQ) and the [Ministry of Energy and Natural Resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Energy_and_Natural_Resources_\(Quebec\)).

The *Ministère de l'Emploi et de la Solidarité sociale du Québec* has the mandate to oversee social and workforce developments through Emploi-Québec and its local employment centres (CLE).[^cite_note-181] This ministry is also responsible for managing the *Régime québécois d'assurance parentale* (QPIP) as well as last-resort financial support for people in need. The [Commission des normes, de l'équité, de la santé et de la sécurité du travail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commission_des_normes,_de_l'%C3%A9quit%C3%A9,_de_la_sant%C3%A9_et_de_la_s%C3%A9curit%C3%A9_du_travail?action=edit\&redlink=1) (CNESST) is the main body responsible for labour laws in Quebec[^cite_note-182] and for enforcing agreements concluded between unions of employees and their employers.[^cite_note-183]

[Revenu Québec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revenu_Qu%C3%A9bec) is the body responsible for collecting taxes.
